<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2020 Aug 25; Accepted 2020 Oct 23; Collection date 2020 Nov.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Mass spectrometry technologies are widely used in the fields of ionomics and metabolomics to simultaneously profile the intracellular concentrations of, e.g., amino acids or elements in genome-wide mutant libraries. These molecular or sub-molecular features are generally non-Gaussian and their covariance reveals patterns of correlations that reflect the system nature of the cell biochemistry and biology. Here, we introduce two similarity measures, the Mahalanobis cosine and the hybrid Mahalanobis cosine, that enforce information from the empirical covariance matrix of omics data from high-throughput screening and that can be used to quantify similarities between the profiled features of different mutants. We evaluate the performance of these similarity measures in the task of inferring and integrating genetic networks from short-profile ionomics/metabolomics data through an analysis of experimental data sets related to the ionome and the metabolome of the model organism <em>S. cerevisiae</em>. The study of the resulting ionome–metabolome <em>Saccharomyces cerevisiae</em> multilayer genetic network, which encodes multiple omic-specific levels of correlations between genes, shows that the proposed measures can provide an alternative description of relations between biological processes when compared to the commonly used Pearson’s correlation coefficient and have the potential to guide the construction of novel hypotheses on the function of uncharacterised genes.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> similarity measures, mahalanobis cosine, multiplex networks, multi-omics integration, ionomics, metabolomics</p></section></section><section id="sec1-metabolites-10-00435"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>The development and reduction in cost of high-throughput technologies in the post-genomic era has made possible genome-wide screening experiments that measure the molecular phenotypes observed in response to single gene alterations, such as deletion, or as a result of an increase in expression of the protein coding sequence [<a href="#B1-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-metabolites-10-00435">1</a>,<a href="#B2-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-metabolites-10-00435">2</a>,<a href="#B3-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-metabolites-10-00435">3</a>,<a href="#B4-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-metabolites-10-00435">4</a>]. As a consequence, functional omics studies that go beyond the paradigm of functional genomics and that are aimed at investigating genotype–phenotype relations at different omic layers have been carried out. Metabolomic [<a href="#B5-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-metabolites-10-00435">5</a>,<a href="#B6-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-metabolites-10-00435">6</a>,<a href="#B7-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-metabolites-10-00435">7</a>] and ionomic [<a href="#B8-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-metabolites-10-00435">8</a>,<a href="#B9-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-metabolites-10-00435">9</a>] profiling have been combined with high-throughput screening of yeast <em>S. cerevisiae</em> single-gene deletion libraries to quantify elements and amino acids (essential building blocks of the cell whose concentration is highly informative on the physiological state in response to genetic perturbations), and to subsequently assess correlations between different mutants on the basis of the measured profiles of biological features. Because these molecular or sub-molecular signatures can be mapped and associated to a consistent region of the genome, statistical inference techniques are often applied to extract genetic networks from correlations that will reflect the interplay between gene function, molecular signatures, and environmental factors. Among other methods such as Bayesian networks [<a href="#B10-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-metabolites-10-00435">10</a>,<a href="#B11-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-metabolites-10-00435">11</a>] and Gaussian graphical models [<a href="#B12-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-metabolites-10-00435">12</a>,<a href="#B13-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-metabolites-10-00435">13</a>,<a href="#B14-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-metabolites-10-00435">14</a>] reconstruction, relevance network inference [<a href="#B15-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-metabolites-10-00435">15</a>,<a href="#B16-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-metabolites-10-00435">16</a>,<a href="#B17-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-metabolites-10-00435">17</a>,<a href="#B18-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-metabolites-10-00435">18</a>] is the most used technique, and it is based on the idea of enforcing a pairwise similarity/distance measure between genes in order to extract via similarity-based thresholding a network of genetic associations on which the modern tools and analysis techniques of network theory [<a href="#B19-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-10-00435">19</a>,<a href="#B20-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-metabolites-10-00435">20</a>,<a href="#B21-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-metabolites-10-00435">21</a>] can be applied to reveal functional patterns at a system level. To quantify similarities, the Pearson’s correlation coefficient [<a href="#B22-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-metabolites-10-00435">22</a>,<a href="#B23-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-metabolites-10-00435">23</a>] is commonly adopted, regardless of the assumptions that the data have to satisfy for it to be used (such as approximate normality and the absence of outliers). This is a practice that is justified by a consensus that its drawbacks are mainly theoretical and that it turns out to be useful in practice in many applications and analyses of real-world data sets [<a href="#B24-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-metabolites-10-00435">24</a>,<a href="#B25-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-metabolites-10-00435">25</a>,<a href="#B26-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-metabolites-10-00435">26</a>,<a href="#B27-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-metabolites-10-00435">27</a>]. However, comprehensive functional omic approaches that target and simultaneously measure a substantial fraction of the intracellular elements (ionomics), or a complete class of metabolites, (e.g., amino acids) [<a href="#B7-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-metabolites-10-00435">7</a>], provide a number of features <em>M</em> profiled for each mutant that is much smaller than the total number <em>N</em> of mutants in the library (<em>N</em> order of the genome size), mathematically <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm1"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>. In this paper, we discuss the potential pitfalls of using the Pearson correlation coefficient in this short-profile omics scenario. We propose two extensions of the cosine similarity, namely the <em>Mahalanobis cosine</em> similarity and the <em>hybrid Mahalanobis cosine</em> similarity that appear more suitable for quantifying phenotype similarities between deletion mutants in the applications described here. Starting from theoretical considerations on the characteristic structure of short-profile omic data, we develop and apply a methodology to quantify advantages these measures may have in the task of extracting biologically meaningful genetic networks. We do this by considering three experimental benchmark data sets of the ionome and the metabolome of the model organism <em>S. cerevisiae</em> and several large curated databases as ground truth for genetic annotations. Our testing procedure for the similarity measures evaluates two fundamental aspects of the process of information extraction that accompanies the inference of a genetic network, namely the performance in encoding relevant biological relationships in the resulting network topology, and the ability to capture potential new biological information when integrating networks from different omic layers.</p></section><section id="sec2-metabolites-10-00435"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Similarity Measures for Short Omic Profiles</h3>
<p>Let <em>Z</em> be an <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm2"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> matrix describing <em>N</em> genome-scale observations of a set of <em>M</em> biological signatures (features)—for example, the intracellular concentration of <em>M</em> amino acids in <em>N</em> different mutant strains of <em>S.cerevisiae</em> in which a single open reading frame (ORF) has been deleted, so that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm3"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>. Let’s assume that these features are standardised, meaning that for each amino acid <em>j</em>, the distribution of intracellular concentrations observed across the genome has zero-mean and unit variance:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula1"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="mm4" display="block"><mrow><mtable displaystyle="true"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mi>N</mi></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>∀</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><msub><mi>σ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac></msqrt><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>∀</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>For each molecular signature <em>f</em>, we can define a genome-wide feature vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm5"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow></math></span> (columns of the matrix <em>Z</em>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD1-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm6" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>N</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(1)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
and since each mutant strain is associated to a specific gene <em>g</em> in the genome, we can write for each gene its associated profile (rows of <em>Z</em>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD2-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm7" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The distribution of the intracellular concentration values of amino acids and other metabolism-related biomolecules measured across a collection of all possible specific mutants (single-gene knockout or overexpression) is usually centred around a typical value corresponding to the wild type phenotype (most of the mutations are neutral and produce a phenotype consistent with the one of the wild type), and exhibits long tails due to high/low concentration values associated to those minority of mutations that alter the cell function and produce a phenotype detected at the level of the molecular profile (examples of these distributions are shown in <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A1" class="usa-link">Figure A1</a>, <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A2" class="usa-link">Figure A2</a> and <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A3" class="usa-link">Figure A3</a>). Moreover the concentrations of these biomolecules are likely to vary in a correlated fashion because they are regulated by the network of metabolic reactions and functional processes within the cell. Therefore, we assume for our omic data set that the standardised feature vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm8"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> are skewed (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm9"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>) and that some of them can be highly correlated (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm10"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD3-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm11" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>N</mi></mfrac><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>v</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>f</mi></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><msup><mrow><mo accent="false">[</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>v</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>f</mi></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo accent="false">]</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></msup></mfrac><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>∀</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
and
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD4-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm12" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mn>2</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>r</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mrow><msqrt><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>f</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></msqrt><msqrt><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi></mrow><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">v</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></msqrt></mrow></mfrac><mo>|</mo></mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mrow><mi>for</mi><mi>some</mi></mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>f</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>A way to put hypothesis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm13"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> in practical terms is to say that, when measuring from the data the feature–feature covariance matrix <em>C</em>, we expect to find a substantial fraction of the matrix elements to be significantly larger than zero in absolute value; therefore, the features reveal a significant and extended pattern of pairwise correlations (examples of these patterns can be seen is <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/d336e6d40558/metabolites-10-00435-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="224" width="730" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Patterns of feature–feature correlations observed in the feature correlation matrix (Pearson coefficient) for the three experimental benchmark data sets considered in this study. In the ionome data sets, the features correspond to intracellular concentrations of different elements profiled in diverse <em>S. cerevisiae</em> mutant strains, while in the metabolome data set, the features correspond to the intracellular concentrations of amino acids.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Taking into account these factors, each element of a generic profile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm14"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span>, corresponding to the concentration value of a specific amino acid, is associated with a distinct, characteristic non-Gaussian, skewed empirical distribution, and the values in the profile array are correlated. It follows that the average value of the profile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm15"><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> is likely to be substantially different from zero for a consistent fraction of mutants showing a phenotype (see <a href="#app3-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix C</a> for a systematic analysis of the average profile value on simulated short-profile omic data). Therefore, if our goal is to find a reliable pairwise similarity score between mutant profiles to infer genetic associations from the data, the Pearson correlation coefficient (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm16"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>) [<a href="#B22-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-metabolites-10-00435">22</a>,<a href="#B23-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-metabolites-10-00435">23</a>] might not be the best choice. For two genetic profiles <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm17"><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> the similarity in terms of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm18"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is given by:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD5-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm19" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mrow><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm20"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is intrinsically multivariate (because of the arithmetic mean <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm21"><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span>), and it should be applied under the assumption that the vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm22"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> contains uncorrelated values that are consistent with the same marginal Gaussian distribution. When we drop the multivariate terms, we have the cosine similarity, defined as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD6-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm23" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></msqrt><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>If we see <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm24"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow></math></span> as a generic vector in an <em>M</em>-dimensional space, then <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm25"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the angle between the two vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm26"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm27"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup></mrow></math></span>. If our features <em>f</em> were Gaussian-like, standardised, and uncorrelated, then the values of the gene profiles would also be normally distributed, and in that scenario, for most of the genes, we would have <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm28"><mrow><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>≃</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm29"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>≃</mo><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>; therefore, there would be no reason to prefer the cosine over the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm30"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and vice versa (see <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A4" class="usa-link">Figure A4</a> and <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A6" class="usa-link">Figure A6</a> for a comparison between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm31"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>- and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm32"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>-generated similarity scores from real short-profile omic data sets and from synthetic short-profile omic data).</p>
<p>The cosine similarity formula contains the Euclidean norm in the denominator; indeed, all Euclidean vector spaces are inner product spaces in which the notion of <em>angle</em> between two generic vectors is well defined (for more details, see <a href="#app1-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix A</a>). Now we consider the following metric, the Mahalanobis distance [<a href="#B28-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-metabolites-10-00435">28</a>], defined as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD7-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm33" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mi>T</mi></msup><msup><mi>C</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>C</em> is the feature–feature covariance matrix estimated from the data. Since any covariance matrix is always semi-positive definite, it is easy to show (see <a href="#app1-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix A</a>) that the Mahalanobis metric induces an inner product space and that it is perfectly legitimate to write the following Mahalanobis cosine similarity:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD8-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm34" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mrow><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm35"><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow></math></span> are the elements of the profile vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm36"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> in the base where the covariance matrix is diagonal with eigenvalues <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm37"><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>. We found one application of the Mahalanobis cosine in computer vision (face recognition) [<a href="#B29-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-metabolites-10-00435">29</a>] and, to our knowledge, this measure has never been applied in the field of bioinformatics or computational biology before. In this paper, we show that it is a suitable and powerful measure to extract genetic association networks from short-profile omic data sets. As an analogue to the <em>pseudo-cosine</em> [<a href="#B30-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-metabolites-10-00435">30</a>], we also introduce the hybrid Mahalanobis cosine similarity, defined as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm38" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mrow><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mi>j</mi></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm39"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is not a proper cosine function because the inner product in the formula corresponds to the Euclidean dot product while the norms are computed with the Mahalanobis norm. Therefore, in principle, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm40"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is not bounded between −1 and 1. However, for practical applications, it is always possible to rebound the scores in the range [−1, 1] by dividing all the scores extracted from a given data set by the largest absolute score value. Equations (<a href="#FD8-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">8</a>) and (<a href="#FD9-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">9</a>) show how the two proposed similarity measures enforce information from the empirical feature–feature covariance matrix extracted from the entire set of data (in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm41"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> framework, the estimation of this matrix is accurate). When computing each pairwise similarity score between profiles, these measures take into account the geometry of the cloud of data points in the <em>M</em>-dimensional feature space (the covariance eigenvalues can be seen to describe a hyper-ellipse with axes of lengths <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm42"><mrow><mrow><mo>{</mo><msub><mi>λ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>), and, in analogy with the general relativity formalism [<a href="#B28-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-metabolites-10-00435">28</a>], they dilate distances by the factors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm43"><mrow><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> so as to penalise the directions in the feature space along which the covariance is low and the data points are less scattered.</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Omic Benchmark Data Sets</h3>
<p>Throughout the paper, we evaluate and compare the performance of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm44"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm45"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm46"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm47"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> in inferring genetic relevance networks from short-profile omic data sets. In particular, we focus on testing these measures in two fundamental tasks of omics data analysis, namely the retrieval of known biological information, and the detection of potential undiscovered functional associations between genes. To do that, we consider here three experimental benchmark data sets:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">(1)</span><p class="display-inline">Yeast ionome ko (non-essential ORF knock-out mutants, iHUB [<a href="#B31-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-metabolites-10-00435">31</a>,<a href="#B32-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-metabolites-10-00435">32</a>,<a href="#B33-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-metabolites-10-00435">33</a>]).</p>
<p>The yeast ionome knock-out (ko) data set contains population-average intracellular concentrations of 14 different elements (Ca, Cd, Co, Cu, Fe, K, Mg, Mn, Mo, Na, Ni, P, S, and Zn) quantified by means of inductively coupled plasma–mass spectrometry (ICP–MS) for a library of 4944 <em>S. cerevisiae</em> haploid mutant strains having a single non-essential open reading frame knocked out.This data set includes a total of 26,976 samples measured in 305 different plates. Most of the strains were measured in replicates of four (4207), 684 strains in replicates of eight, 48 strains in replicates of 12, and two strains in replicates of 16. Additionally, three control strains present in multiple trays were included in our analysis: YDL227C, 1620 replicates; YLR396C, 1224 replicates; YPR065W, 1224 replicates.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(2)</span><p class="display-inline">Yeast ionome oe (overexpression mutants, iHUB [<a href="#B31-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-metabolites-10-00435">31</a>,<a href="#B32-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-metabolites-10-00435">32</a>,<a href="#B33-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-metabolites-10-00435">33</a>]).</p>
<p>The yeast ionome overexpression (oe) data set contains population-average intracellular concentrations of 17 different elements (As, Ca, Cd, Cl, Co, Cu, Fe, K, Mg, Mn, Mo, Na, Ni, P, S, Se, and Zn) quantified by means of inductively coupled plasma–mass spectrometry (ICP–MS) for a library of 5718 <em>S. cerevisiae</em> haploid mutant strains having a single essential or non-essential open reading frame overexpressed. This data set includes a total of 24,060 samples measured in 310 different plates. Most of the strains were measured in replicates of four (5426), 287 strains in replicates of eight, and five strains in replicates of 12.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(3)</span><p class="display-inline">Yeast metabolome aa (amino acid profile of non-essential ORF knock-out mutants [<a href="#B7-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-metabolites-10-00435">7</a>]).</p>
<p>The yeast metabolome (aa) data set contains population-average intracellular concentrations of 19 different amino acids (A, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, M, N, P, Q, R, S, T, V, W, Y) quantified by means of liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC–MS) for a library of 4475 <em>S. cerevisiae</em> haploid mutant strains having a single non-essential open reading frame knocked out. This data set includes a total of 4653 samples measured in 12 different batches. Most of the strains are associated with a single sample (4324), 128 strains have two replicates, 19 strains have three replicates, and four strains have four replicates.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Information and details concerning these data sets, including quality control analyses, can be found in [<a href="#B7-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-metabolites-10-00435">7</a>,<a href="#B33-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-metabolites-10-00435">33</a>,<a href="#B34-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-metabolites-10-00435">34</a>]. To allow for an unbiased comparison of the different measures performance across the different data sets, all data are processed using the same pipeline starting from the raw measured concentration values. The pipeline corrects the raw data for batch effects and extracts a characteristic concentration profile for each strain by sequentially applying the following operations: (i) log-transformation of the data, (ii) median plate normalisation, (iii) outlier detection and removal, (iv) extraction of a median profile from the replicates (where replicates are available) for each strain, and (v) standardisation of the concentration values (a detailed description of the pipeline can be found in <a href="#app2-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix B</a>). For each data set, the final mutant-related feature profiles show how many standard deviations the concentrations deviate from the median concentration measured across all the strains in that data set. These data sets have been obtained using a similar experimental design, and they also present the general characteristic of short-profile omic data sets discussed in <a href="#sec2dot1-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Section 2.1</a>: in <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, we report the average absolute feature skewness (AAFS) and the number-of-features over number-of-genes ratio <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm48"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> (after data processing); in <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>, we show the patterns of feature–feature correlations extracted from each experimental data set using the Pearson correlation coefficient (note that in the the framework <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm49"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, the feature–feature covariance matrix can be reliably estimated from the empirical correlation matrix).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Characteristics of the benchmark data sets considered in the study (after processing raw data). </table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/metabolites-10-00435-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec2dot3-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Genetic Networks Inference</h3>
<p>The statistic of pairwise similarity score values depends in general on the specific similarity measure used to calculate the scores. In order to robustly compare the topology of the genetic relevance networks obtained with the different similarity measures of interest (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm50"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm51"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm52"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm53"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>) we adopt a rank-based thresholding of the scores that depends in turn on the more robust rank statistic of the scores. Our general inference methodology consists of the following steps:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>Score computation: we compute all pairwise similarity scores between the <em>N</em> genes of a given data set with each similarity measure of interest.</p></li>
<li><p>Score ranking: we rank in descending value order each set of scores computed with a different similarity measure.</p></li>
<li><p>Relevance network extraction: we retain the top-<em>n</em> ranked scores in each set to define for each similarity measure a genetic association network of <em>N</em> nodes and <em>n</em> links; the links correspond to the <em>n</em> highest values in the score rank statistic.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This procedure allows for a robust comparative analysis of the genetic networks inferred using the different measures at any fixed value of the relevance threshold <em>n</em>. In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>, we show the topology of the genetic relevance networks inferred from the ionome (ko) data set for different values of the relevance threshold <em>n</em> when using <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm54"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm55"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm56"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>. To improve visualisation, we plot only the connected subgraphs with a size larger than or equal to five nodes. An analysis of the false positive rate of associations for the inferred relevance networks is reported in <a href="#app4-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix D</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/3031b9869e2a/metabolites-10-00435-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="473" width="775" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Genetic relevance networks extracted from the ionome knock-out data set using three different values of the relevance threshold <em>n</em>. For a given threshold value, <em>n</em> links included in the network correspond to the top <em>n</em> pairwise similarity scores measured between the genes. To improve visualisation, we display the connected subgraphs with a size larger than or equal to five nodes when <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm194"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm195"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm196"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> are used to measure the scores, respectively. The evolution of the network topology resulting from the inclusion of more and more links for decreasing <em>n</em> values can be regarded as a reverse percolation process on the network.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot4-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Multiplex Integration of Genetic Networks</h3>
<p>Once we have defined the procedure to extract genetic networks from a single omic data set, we can integrate, within the multilayer network paradigm, the genetic associations from all three benchmark data sets. In particular, our integration methodology will focus on constructing multiplex networks [<a href="#B35-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-metabolites-10-00435">35</a>,<a href="#B36-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-metabolites-10-00435">36</a>,<a href="#B37-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-metabolites-10-00435">37</a>,<a href="#B38-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-metabolites-10-00435">38</a>]. For each of the similarity measures of interest, we will take the set of all genes profiled in at least one data set and we will construct a multiplex network of three layers, each containing the single network inferred from one of the data sets by retaining the top 100,000 most relevant associations.</p>
<p>A multiplex network <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm57"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">G</mi></mrow></math></span> is mathematically defined by a set of nodes <em>V</em> and <em>K</em> single-network layers <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm58"><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>E</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm59"><mrow><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>K</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm60"><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>α</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> indicating the set of links in layer <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm61"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> (see <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). The multiplex network framework allows us to efficiently encode associations between genes for which there is omic-specific evidence or evidence across multiple omic layers by means of the <em>multilink</em> concept [<a href="#B38-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-metabolites-10-00435">38</a>,<a href="#B39-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-metabolites-10-00435">39</a>,<a href="#B40-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-metabolites-10-00435">40</a>]. A multilink that connects a pair of nodes <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm62"><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is defined by a vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm63"><mrow><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo></mover><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>m</mi><mn>2</mn><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>m</mi><mi>K</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span> which specifies all the layers <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm64"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> where those nodes are connected (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm65"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>m</mi><mi>α</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>), and where they are not (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm66"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>m</mi><mi>α</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>). The multilinks can characterise all the different ways in which a pair of nodes in the multiplex can be connected across the <em>K</em> layers. For example <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm67"><mrow><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo></mover><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span> indicates that node <em>i</em> and <em>j</em> are connected in layer 1 only, while in the case <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm68"><mrow><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo></mover><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>, they are connected in both layer 1 and 3. It is then possible to define the <em>multidegree</em>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm69"><mrow><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo></mover></msubsup></mrow></math></span> of a generic node <em>i</em> as the number of neighbours that are connected via multilinks of type <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm70"><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/0b696d9d0070/metabolites-10-00435-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="319" width="782" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Diagram illustrating the concept of the <em>S. cerevisiae</em> ionome–metabolome multiplex genetic network. By means of mass-spectrometry technology, it is possible to profile the intracellular concentration of amino acids and elements in yeast mutant strains that have one single open reading frame (ORF) deleted or overexpressed (loss or amplification of single gene function). By quantifying similarities between mutant profiles, a genetic network can be extracted from each high-throughput screening and the single omic networks can be integrated into a multilayer network where the same set of nodes (corresponding to almost all non-essential ORFs) is connected on different layers, each describing connections between genes whose loss/overexpression produce a similar phenotypic response at the level of the metabolome or ionome.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="sec3-metabolites-10-00435"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<p>The process of inference of a genetic network from a given data set while gradually increasing the relevance threshold (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>) can be regarded as a reverse percolation process on the network, in which edges are sequentially added one at a time between the same set of <em>N</em> vertices [<a href="#B41-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-metabolites-10-00435">41</a>,<a href="#B42-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-metabolites-10-00435">42</a>]. This fact allows us to obtain insights about the inference power and performance of the similarity measures based on network theory considerations. Our first analysis focuses on three network descriptors, namely (i) the number of connected components—that is, the number of connected subgraphs in the network; (ii) the size of the largest component (LC)—that is, the number of nodes present in the largest connected component of the graph; and (iii) the average local clustering coefficient [<a href="#B43-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-10-00435">43</a>,<a href="#B44-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-metabolites-10-00435">44</a>] of the largest component—that is, the average fraction of triangles over triplets closed by a node in the LC. The clustering coefficient measures how well the nodes in a graph tend to cluster together, and it works as an indicator for the network modularity [<a href="#B45-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-metabolites-10-00435">45</a>,<a href="#B46-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-metabolites-10-00435">46</a>,<a href="#B47-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-metabolites-10-00435">47</a>]. In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>, we plot the number of connected components, the size of the LC, and the average clustering coefficient of the LC measured from the inferred networks in the top <em>n</em> genetic associations ranked according to the different measures considered. Interestingly, the curves reveal that:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>By increasing the relevance threshold <em>n</em> and considering more and more links in the inferred networks, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm71"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> tends to generate faster than <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm72"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and than <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm73"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> a giant component whose node size is a finite fraction of the genome size <em>N</em> (yellow curves in top and middle panels, the number of connected components goes to one, and the size of the LC increases asymptotically to <em>N</em>), with a relatively (comparable to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm74"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm75"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>) low level of clustering of the nodes (bottom panels).</p></li>
<li><p>On the other hand, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm76"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> tends to aggregate genes in few connected components or modules (purple curves, top panels) which grow in parallel with relatively comparable sizes (middle panels, the size of the LC is far below <em>N</em>), and are highly clustered (purple marks in bottom panels, high clustering coefficient).</p></li>
</ul>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f004"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/bce098a2278d/metabolites-10-00435-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="460" width="797" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Percolation analysis of the relevance network inference process carried out with all the similarity measures considered, on all experimental data sets under study. (<strong>Top</strong>) we plot for each data set the evolution of the number of connected components in the extracted networks in function of the number of links <em>n</em> inferred using the top <em>n</em> similarity scores. (<strong>Middle</strong>) We also show the evolution of the size of the largest component (LC) divided by the total number of genes <em>N</em> in function of the number of top <em>n</em> scores. (<strong>Bottom</strong>) We quantify the average local clustering coefficient of the nodes in the largest component (LC) when different orders of magnitude of number of links are inferred in the genetic network. The curves indicate that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm197"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> tends to connect faster than the other measures all the genes in the network in one connected component from many small subgraphs, while, on the contrary, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm198"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> tends to infer links which characterise relations between genes within few large connected components which are comparable in node size with respect to the largest component and reveal a highly clustered architecture in terms of associations.</p></figcaption></figure><p>This analysis suggests that the measures that enforce information from the feature covariance matrix, namely <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm77"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm78"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, represent two alternative ways of operating at the genome scale: given a certain number <em>n</em> of reliable associations, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm79"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is able to infer relations that involve a larger set of genes, thus retrieving faster biological information at the global genetic network scale, while <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm80"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> can best retrieve information at the local genetic network scale by selecting associations within several isolated clusters of genes.</p>
<section id="sec3dot1-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Retrieval of Known Biological Information</h3>
<p>The above considerations are based on the assumption that the links inferred contain relevant biological information. We therefore designed a testing regime that quantifies the performance of the proposed measures in the task of retrieving previously known biological information. By assuming that a certain amount of known biological information is contained in each of the benchmark data sets, we want to quantify which similarity measure can better recall that information. We consider separately five curated databases as ground-truth for genetic associations:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">(1)</span><p class="display-inline">Protein–Protein Interactions (PPI) from STRING [<a href="#B48-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-metabolites-10-00435">48</a>] (v11, database scores only).</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(2)</span><p class="display-inline">BIOGRID [<a href="#B49-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-metabolites-10-00435">49</a>] (v3.4.158, genetic interactions only).</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(3)</span><p class="display-inline">Protein Complex Consensus [<a href="#B50-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-metabolites-10-00435">50</a>] (co-occurrence in protein complexes).</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(4)</span><p class="display-inline">KEGG Biological Pathways [<a href="#B51-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-metabolites-10-00435">51</a>] (Release 90.1, co-occurrence in metabolic pathways).</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(5)</span><p class="display-inline">Yeast Net Gold Standard [<a href="#B52-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-metabolites-10-00435">52</a>] (v3, Gene Ontology based gold-standard associations).</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>For each of the benchmark data sets, we compute the percentage of corresponding ground-truth links that are found in the associated networks (true positive rate, or <em>recall</em>) extracted at different values of the relevance threshold <em>n</em>, and compare those numbers across the similarity measures of interest used in the inference process. Of note, this approach is conceptually different from a receiver operating characteristic curve analysis (a study of the true positive associations rate against false negative associations rate in function of the threshold <em>n</em>), that would evaluate the diagnostic ability of each omic data set in recovering each specific type of biological associations alone. For any set of ground-truth associations (single database), such analysis would disregard any amount of potential new information extracted from a given data set (which is highly dependent on the specific type of omic data considered and on the associated experimental conditions—for example, the type of media used to grow the yeast mutants) as well as information that is relative to other databases.</p>
<p>Results are shown in <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>. For the top <em>n</em> scores values considered, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm81"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mn>1000</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>000</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>100</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>000</mn><mo>]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, the true positive rate (TPR) of retrieved associations is reported. The red stars mark the measures that performed best with at least a 10% gain in performance with respect to the second best (that is, the difference in TPR value between the best measure and second best measure divided by the second best TPR). In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, we report details of the best recall performance with its associated performance gain. Overall, the true positive rates are very low, which is expected because it is hard to reconstruct the exact ground truth networks, especially using 1000 or 10,000 top scores; however, the estimated false positive rates of associations are at least an order of magnitude smaller (see <a href="#app4-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">Appendix D</a>). In the case of the metabolome (aa), which revealed the most extended and prominent correlation pattern across the set of features (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm82"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> appeared to consistently outperform the other metrics in recalling protein–protein interactions, protein complexes associations, and biological pathways co-occurrences (links between genes annotated to the same metabolic pathway). In the case of the ionome data sets, in which the level of correlation between features was found to be lower, we observed that for the top 100,000 associations considered, the recalling of the similarity measures is comparable; however, when considering lower relevance thresholds (1000 and 10,000 associations), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm83"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm84"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> outperformed the other measures in several cases. For the ionome (ko), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm85"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm86"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> consistently performed best in recalling information about protein complexes and biological pathways, respectively. In particular, when only a few highly reliable associations were considered (top 1000 scores), the gain in performance of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm87"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm88"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> was often extremely high (see <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). The Pearson correlation coefficient appeared to perform effectively better only in one single case (PPI, top 10,000 scores).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/6fa0c791842f/metabolites-10-00435-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="415" width="737" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Comparison in performance of the similarity measures under study in recalling previously known biology. Protein–protein interactions (PPI), co-occurrence in protein complexes, co-occurrence in metabolic pathways (KEGG), genetic interactions (BIOGRID), and associations based on Gene Ontology (GO) Terms (YeastNet GS) were considered separately as ground-truth for genetic associations. For each experimental data set of ionome/metabolome, the true positive rate of associations found in the top 1000 scores (<strong>Top</strong>), in the top 10,000 scores (<strong>Middle</strong>), and in the top 100,000 scores (<strong>Bottom</strong>) is reported, respectively, for each of the similarity measures under study. The red stars mark the cases in which the highest true positive rate differs by at least 10% with respect to the second highest. We considered this difference in percentage as the gain in performance associated to the best performing measure. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm199"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> appears to consistently outperform the other measures in recalling protein–protein interactions, protein complexes associations, and biological pathways co-occurrence in the networks extracted from the metabolome (aa) data set.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-t002"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Best performing similarity measures and associated gain in performance in the task of recalling associations from protein–protein interactions (PPI), co-occurrence in protein complexes, co-occurrence in metabolic pathways (KEGG), genetic interactions (BIOGRID), and associations based on GO Ontology Terms (YeastNet GS).The gain in performance is defined as the difference in percentage between the best and the second best true positive rate obtained with the measures under study on a data set.</p></div>
