<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/metabolites-10-00435-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec3dot2-metabolites-10-00435"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. The Ionome–Metabolome Multiplex Genetic Network of the Yeast <em>S. cerevisiae</em>
</h3>
<p>When we constructed the multiplex network from the three single-network layers inferred from each data set using the top 100,000 significant associations, we observed that the multilink statistics did not differ in terms of orders of magnitude across the similarity measures considered (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>A), with the exception of the multilinks of the type <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em> for which <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm89"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> provided the largest number of links (2537) and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm90"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> the lowest (685). Each measure, however, had a substantial fraction of characteristic multilinks that did not overlap with the other measures (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>B). The lowest specificity was observed for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm91"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> with only 26.8% of characteristic <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks, while the highest specificity was observed with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm92"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> (respectively 92.9% of <em>ionome.ko-ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks and 77.8% of <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks that are not in common with other measures). Furthermore, we checked the content of relevant biological information of non-layer-specific (NLS) multilinks by considering a subset of GOSlim Biological Process Ontology terms from the SGD database [<a href="#B53-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-metabolites-10-00435">53</a>] that are related to metabolic processes and cellular ion homeostasis maintenance [<a href="#B34-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-metabolites-10-00435">34</a>,<a href="#B54-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-metabolites-10-00435">54</a>]. When we looked at the four subnetworks defined by each specific class of NLS multilinks, we found that those constructed with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm93"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> included the largest number of genes annotated to the ontology categories considered (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>C). <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm94"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> also yielded the largest number of <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks between the annotated genes considered, while for the two multilinks classes <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em> and <em>ionome.ko-ionome.oe</em>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm95"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> revealed the largest number of connections among the annotated genes (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>A).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/b206402f90dd/metabolites-10-00435-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="524" width="783" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Analysis of the <em>S. cerevisiae ionome–metabolome multiplex genetic network</em>. Using each of the similarity measures under study, we construct a multiplex network by superimposing the three networks defined by the top 100,000 similarity scores in the ionome (ko), ionome (oe), and metabolome (aa), respectively. (Panel <strong>A</strong>) The statistics of non-layer-specific multilinks compared across the multiplex networks obtained with the different measures under study. (Panel <strong>B</strong>) Venn diagrams showing the overlap of non-layer-specific multilinks between the different measures under study. (Panel <strong>C</strong>) We show the percentage of genes annotated to a selected subset of GOSlim biological processes related to metabolic processes and cellular ion homeostasis maintenance that are connected in the non-layer-specific multilinks subnetwork of the different measures. In panel A, the number of multilinks of different types that are incident in these selected genes is also highlighted (red solid lines).</p></figcaption></figure><p>These results obtained in the multiplex framework were consistent with the previous results and supported the observation that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm96"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> best captures global information at the genome scale by spanning in its NLS subnetworks the largest number of annotated genes, while <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm97"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> could reveal larger, well-clustered neighbourhoods of fewer annotated genes, thus better characterising them at a local network scale. The proposed measures also revealed alternative relations between different biological processes compared to the Pearson’s correlation coefficient. In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>, the flow of multilinks between the different ontology classes selected for the <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm98"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> subnetwork and for the <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm99"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> subnetwork is depicted, together with the analogous <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm100"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> flows. The large number of genetic associations belonging to these two NLS multilink types (see <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>A) also reflected a richer scenario in terms of the flow of multilinks between biological processes. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm101"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> revealed, for example, associations between ion transport genes and genes annotated to endosomal transport, nucleobase-containing compound transport, and vacuole organization, while <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm102"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> revealed associations between Golgi vesicle transport genes and several ontologies including Golgi vesicle transport, endosomal transport, ion transport, mitochondrion organization, nucleobase-containing compound transport, transmembrane transport, the cellular amino acid metabolic process, generation of precursor metabolites and energy, and the lipid metabolic process that were not captured by the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm103"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f007"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/d0ed4833654a/metabolites-10-00435-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="614" width="731" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Flow diagrams representing the proportion of multilinks between genes annotated to selected GOSlim classes of biological processes related to ion transport and metabolism. The proposed similarity measures are able to reveal new levels of functional associations between genes: (<strong>Top</strong>) The flows generated by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm200"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm201"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> for the <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks are compared. (<strong>Bottom</strong>) The flows generated by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm202"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm203"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> for the <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em> multilinks are compared. Those are the classes of multilinks for which <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm204"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm205"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> respectively produce the highest flow diversity with respect to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm206"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p><a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A provides a clear example of a gene, LST7, whose functional role falls at the interface between metabolic processes and ion homeostasis, that is highlighted by the multilink flow analysis. The <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em> neighbourhood of LST7 spanned by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm104"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A, left) contains GTR1 and MHE1 (EGO1). This triad is known to be involved in the regulation of TOR signalling; indeed, Gtr1 and its membrane-tethering subunits Ego1 are part of the EGO complex that binds the vacuolar membrane and subunits of TORC1. The Lst4-Lst7 complex has been recently shown [<a href="#B55-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-metabolites-10-00435">55</a>] to attach to the vacuolar membrane next to the GTPase complex Gtr1-Gtr2 under amino acid starvation, and to transiently interact with Gtr1-Gtr2, thereby entailing TORC1 activation and Lst4-Lst7 release from the membrane, under subsequent amino acids stimulation (for example, glutamine). This machinery is reflected by the significantly altered amino acid profiles of the genes (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A, bottom-right) with an overabundance of several species, including glutamine, and none of the ions overrepresented (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A, top-right), suggesting that the loss of function of these genes induces in the cell a state of stationary amino acids stimulation. The ion profiles also shows a significant decrease in sodium concentration in the cell that reflects an interplay between the amino acid induced signalling of TORC1 and pH homeostasis: physical interactions from high-throughput data of the protein-fragment complementation assay [<a href="#B56-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-10-00435">56</a>] indeed suggest association of Ego1 and Gtr1 with Vma8, the D subunit of the V1 peripheral membrane domain of the vacuolar ATPase (V-ATPase) that is essential for maintaining ion homeostasis. In humans, Gtr1 signalling has also been shown to depend on interactions with the vacuolar V-ATPase [<a href="#B57-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-metabolites-10-00435">57</a>,<a href="#B58-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-metabolites-10-00435">58</a>]).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-f008"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/95979af26aa7/metabolites-10-00435-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="360" width="741" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Examples of genes used for hypothesis construction via multiplex neighbourhood analysis. Only non-layer-specific multilinks are plotted, as they represent evidence detected across multiple omic layers (respectively, <em>ionome.ko-ionome.oe</em>, <em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em>, and <em>ionome.oe-metabolome.aa</em>). The non-layer-specific multilink neighbourhood of genes LST7 in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm207"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex (Panel <strong>A</strong>), QDR2 in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm208"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex (Panel <strong>B</strong>), TPI1 in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm209"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex (Panel <strong>C</strong>), and GCS1 in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm210"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex (Panel <strong>D</strong>) are shown together with their ion/amino acid profiles.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Finally, in order to illustrate how enforcing these measures can help construct novel hypotheses on the function of uncharacterised genes, we selected examples of genes from the characteristic flows discussed above that contained in their network neighbourhood genes of unknown function (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>B–D). The <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm105"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>
<em>ionome.ko-metabolome.aa</em> neighbourhood of the plasma membrane transport gene QDR2 (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>B, left) suggests an association with the plasma membrane gene SYG1 of unknown function which is also connected with the uncharacterised membrane protein YIL054W. Interestingly, the knockout ionome profiles of QDR2 (which is known to contribute to potassium homeostasis and whose expression is regulated by copper), SYG1, and YIL054W display evident signatures including a characteristic decrease in cadmium and an increase in sulphur, and variable levels of accumulation of manganese and sodium and decumulation of molybdenum. The metabolic profiles show a general overabundance of all amino acid species including leucine and lysine. When we performed transcription factor enrichment analysis (YeastEnricher online tool [<a href="#B59-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-metabolites-10-00435">59</a>]), we consistently found significant regulation by CAD1, a cadmium resistance gene, involved in stress responses and iron metabolism (adjusted <em>p</em>-value 0.04956) and LEU3, involved in amino acids biosynthesis, (adjusted <em>p</em>-value 0.04956). The QDR2 neighbourhood also contains KGD1, which is associated with LEU3 and annotated to the <em>lysine degradation</em> pathway (KEGG 2018), and FKH1, which is annotated to <em>DNA-binding transcription activator activity, RNA polymerase II-specific</em> together with CAD1 and LEU3, suggesting that SYG1 and YIL054W might take part in membrane transport processes involving QDR2, that are orchestrated by LEU3 and CAD1 according to cellular amino acids and cations availability.</p>
<p>Another interesting example is gene TPI1 (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>C), a glycolytic enzyme which contains in its <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm106"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex neighbourhood the uncharacterised proteins YDR514C and YMR102C and the transport gene ATP19, subunit of the the mitochondrial F1F0 ATP synthase. The mRNA half-life of TPI1 is regulated by iron availability which is decreased in the ion knock-out profiles of the strains while molybdenum is variably decumulated. This behaviour is characteristic of mRNA sequestrated by P-bodies (processing bodies), which are sites where, under stress conditions, nontranslating mRNA is degraded or stored to return back to translated when the cell enters stress recovery [<a href="#B60-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-metabolites-10-00435">60</a>]. TPI1 has been experimentally observed to localise in P-bodies under glucose depletion in <em>S. cerevisiae</em> via chemical cross-linking coupled to affinity purification (cCLAP) [<a href="#B61-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-metabolites-10-00435">61</a>], together with other subunits of the F1F0 ATP synthase complex (ATP11, ATP14, ATP20). YDR514C and YMR102C have been observed to physically interact in <em>S. cerevisiae</em> with Ccr4, the core subunit of the Ccr4-Not complex, which is involved in the regulation of translation and decay of specific mRNAs and is the main cytoplasmic deadenylase in <em>S cerevisiae</em>, and with its associated protein Dhh1 [<a href="#B62-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-metabolites-10-00435">62</a>]. Ccr4 and Dhh1 associate with mRNAs whose abundance increases during nutrient starvation, and those that fluctuate during metabolic and oxygen consumption cycles. Moreover, there is experimental evidence that YMR102C mRNA is sequestrated by P-bodies under glucose stress, Ca <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm107"><mrow><msup><mrow></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> stress, and Na <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm108"><mrow><msup><mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo></msup></mrow></math></span> stress [<a href="#B61-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-metabolites-10-00435">61</a>]. Given that YDR514C, ATP19, and TPI localise to mitochondria, and that inhibition of TPI1 is known to stimulate the pentose phosphate pathway and to increase antioxidative metabolism [<a href="#B63-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-metabolites-10-00435">63</a>], the scenario described suggests the idea that YDR514C and YMR102C, together with TPI1, might be activated in the cell to reshape and switch the metabolism in response to ions/nutrients related stress that might cause mitochondrial disruption and aerobic inefficiency. This hypothesis is supported by the ion overexpression profiles of these genes, in which the concentration level of potential toxic species like Cd and As is very low, as well as by experimental evidence that YMR102C is involved in galactose metabolism in the haploid <em>S cerevisiae</em> Cd-resistant strain (EC9-8, that tolerates high levels of cadmium) [<a href="#B64-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-metabolites-10-00435">64</a>], and significantly overexpressed in the ethanol-tolerant strain Y-50316 [<a href="#B65-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-metabolites-10-00435">65</a>].</p>
<p>As a last example of hypothesis formulation, we considered the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm109"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multiplex of the Golgi transport gene GCS1 (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>D, left). We found that HRT3, YLR352W, and ROY1 physically interact with Skp1 to form SCF-ubiquitin ligase complexes similar to F-box proteins, despite the fact that they lack an identifiable F-box domain [<a href="#B66-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-metabolites-10-00435">66</a>]. Gcs1 contains a ArfGAP1 lipid packing sensor (ALPS) motif that binds to lipid membranes to recruit coat complexes whose role is to generate carrier vesicles that mediate transport of proteins and lipids between intracellular compartments. This ALPS motif couples the activity of GCS1 with the curvature of lipid membranes and allows GCS1 to control the assembly and the dynamics of the COPI coat complex, analogously to its human homologue Arf1GAP1 [<a href="#B67-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-metabolites-10-00435">67</a>]. Interestingly, NUP133, which is found in the neighbourhood of GCS1, is also a membrane curvature sensors and it encodes the same ALPS motif in the Nup84p subcomplex of the nuclear pore complex (NCP) [<a href="#B68-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-metabolites-10-00435">68</a>]. Moreover, it has been shown that the F-box protein Rcy1 is required for recycling of Snc1 to the Golgi, although its precise mechanism is unknown [<a href="#B69-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-metabolites-10-00435">69</a>]. An intriguing hypothesis suggested by the ionome–metabolome multilayer is that HRT3, ROY1, and YLR352W might take part in machinery controlling recycling of plasma membrane proteins similarly to RCY1. The ability of the Rcy1-Skp1 complex to recycle independently of the cullin subunit makes this hypothesis plausible even in the absence of recognised E3 activity by Hrt3, Ylr352w, and Roy1. These pseudo-F-box-Skp1 complexes could be specifically activated through different stress factors. Indeed, other genes in the subnetwork include general regulators of ion homeostasis (boron efflux transporter BOR1 and the potassium transport system TRK1). NVJ1, which promotes the formation of ER–vacuole junctions that can expand in response to starvation and regulate the production of lipid droplets [<a href="#B70-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-metabolites-10-00435">70</a>]. TGL3, whose expression is known to be reduced in the absence of lipid droplets. PDR3, a gene of the conserved pleiotropic drug resistance (PDR) pathway, that, together with PDR1, regulates more than half of the known pumps transporting potentially harmful chemicals outside of the cell membrane, is known to be overexpressed in response to the loss of CCW12 (also present in the subnetwork) which is crucial for wall integrity [<a href="#B71-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-metabolites-10-00435">71</a>]. GLO1 is also of interest because is known to be regulated by osmotic stress and to process glutathione, a sulfur compound that is synthesised in yeast as a cadmium detoxifying agent and whose synthesis has been recently shown to be mediated by an SCF ligase complex (SCF-Met30) [<a href="#B72-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-metabolites-10-00435">72</a>].</p>
<p>As a general remark, besides the proposed hypotheses, the clusters of genes discussed, which are not present in the corresponding <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm110"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> multilayer network (not shown), confirmed that these measures have the potential to reveal aspects of the complex interplay between metabolism, ion homeostasis, and molecular transport, and can be used as novel analytical tools to quantify genetic similarity on the base of the altered levels of nutrients, such as amino acids and ions, that can be used as footprints of the global impact of altered genetic functions (loss or overexpression) in the cell.</p></section></section><section id="sec4-metabolites-10-00435"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p>Network science has played a fundamental role in the development of the field of systems biology by providing analytical tools to reveal and characterise protein and genetic interaction maps and the relations between metabolic pathways and functional landscapes of many biological systems [<a href="#B73-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-metabolites-10-00435">73</a>,<a href="#B74-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-metabolites-10-00435">74</a>,<a href="#B75-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-metabolites-10-00435">75</a>]. However, with the advent of the multi-omics era there is recognition that single isolated biological networks are insufficient to describe functional genetic patterns that arise from the multiple levels of complexity of the cell (genome, epigenome, transcriptome, metabolome, proteome, lipidome, ionome) [<a href="#B76-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-metabolites-10-00435">76</a>]. Network analysis can provide advanced and powerful mathematical frameworks, such as multi-layer networks, to integrate multiple omics data efficiently and in the most intuitive way. In this article, we have focused on the problem of inferring and integrating association networks between genes from omic data sets containing a relatively small number (order <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm111"><mrow><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>(</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>) of biological signatures, profiled for almost all single non-essential gene mutants. These signatures contain comprehensive information on the intracellular concentration of elements or of classes of metabolites, and they present patterns of correlations that reflect those biological and biochemical processes inside the cell in which these concentrations play a fundamental role. The importance of these omic data lies in the fact that the associated studies and methodologies have been proposed as functional omic approaches alternative to the classic functional genomics that can reveal undiscovered relations between genes encoded in the specific omic-related signatures. Extracting informative genetic association networks from these types of short-profile omic data sets is the starting point to apply the tools and algorithms of modern graph theory for revealing new potential functional relations between genes, and from a mathematical perspective this translates into the fundamental issue of assessing reliable similarity scores between the signature vectors. Here, we proposed two pairwise-similarity measures, namely the Mahalanobis cosine, that, to the extent of our knowledge, has never been used before in computational biology, and what we defined as the hybrid Mahalanobis cosine. These two measures can be seen as extensions of the cosine similarity that enforce in different ways additional information from the empirical covariance matrix estimated from the entire set of data under study and can therefore be regarded as providing omic intrinsic or omic adjusted correlations when used to analyse data sets that are at the omic scale. We tested these measures in two fundamental tasks: (1) the inference of genetic relevance networks that can encode in their topology already known biological relationships, and (2) network-based multi-omic integration of short-profile omic data sets, for which multiple evidence of connection across the layers of a multiplex network can indicate potential undiscovered genetic associations. To do that, we developed a methodology that combines extraction and integration of relevance genetic networks based on the robust rank statistic of the similarity scores with cross-referencing from large curated databases of metabolic pathways, genetic interactions, protein–protein interactions, protein complexes co-occurrence, and GO Ontology annotations. We evaluated and compared the performance of the proposed measures against the widely used Pearson correlation coefficient and the standard cosine similarity using three experimental data sets of the ionome and metabolome of the model organism <em>S. cerevisiae</em>. Our construction and analysis of the first ionome–metabolome multiplex genetic network of the yeast <em>S. cerevisiae</em> indicates that the proposed covariance-based similarity measures, when utilised in the tasks of genetic network inference and network-based omics integration, have the potential to capture alternative and/or additional levels of relations between biological pathways and processes, and they can help to elucidate the function of uncharacterised genes through the inferred multilayer network topology.</p>
<p>The pipeline developed to pre-process the data deliberately removed all samples with any outlier or missing value in their concentration profile. Future work will be directed at investigating the robustness of the two measures proposed (Mahalanobis cosine and hybrid Mahalanobis cosine) to the presence of outliers and to missing value imputation. These questions would require, within the short-profile omic data framework, a combined theoretical and computational analysis of the perturbation spectrum of the eigenvalues of the covariance matrix of multivariate distributions with long tails and correlated dimensions.</p>
<p>As a final remark, the similarity measures proposed define corresponding distance measures through the simple transformation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm112"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>s</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>, therefore they can be straightforwardly used in machine learning applications—for example, implemented as alternative metrics into state-of-the-art dimensionality reduction algorithms, such as t-SNE [<a href="#B77-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-metabolites-10-00435">77</a>] and UMAP [<a href="#B78-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-metabolites-10-00435">78</a>], and clustering algorithms, including density-based tools [<a href="#B79-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-10-00435">79</a>,<a href="#B80-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-metabolites-10-00435">80</a>].</p></section><section id="app1-metabolites-10-00435" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Appendix A. Mahalanobis Vector Space</h2>
<p>Let us consider two generic vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm113"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>m</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> in a vector space and write the cosine of the angle between them:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD10-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm114" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A1)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This formula implies that the metric of the space is Euclidean; indeed, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm115"><mrow><msub><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> is the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm116"><mrow><msub><mi>l</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> Euclidean norm. We can write Equation (<a href="#FD10-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">A1</a>) because a vector space with the Euclidean metric is not just a normed vector space, in which the Euclidean norm satisfies the following three properties: </p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula12"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="mm117" display="block"><mrow><mtable displaystyle="true"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>and</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>only if</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>=</mo><mn mathvariant="bold">0</mn></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mo>|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>for any scalar</mi><mtext> </mtext><mi>α</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>≥</mo><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mtext> </mtext><mi>for any vectors</mi><mtext> </mtext><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi>and</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mtext> </mtext><mo>(</mo><mi>triangular inequality</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>
but an inner product space, in which also the parallelogram equality
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD11-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm118" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>−</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
is satisfied for any vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm119"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm120"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi></mrow></math></span> in the space. In fact, the parallelogram equality is a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of a inner product corresponding to a given norm. When Equation (<a href="#FD11-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">A2</a>) holds, the normed space is a vector space with an additional structure, the inner product <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm121"><mrow><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mo>·</mo><mo>&gt;</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, which is defined by the formula
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD12-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm122" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>−</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A3)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
and which naturally induces the associated norm:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD13-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm123" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow></msqrt><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In the case of the Euclidean metric
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula16"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="mm124" display="block"><mrow><mtable displaystyle="true"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>−</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mspace width="1.em"></mspace></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mi>i</mi></msub><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mspace width="1.em"></mspace></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>x</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>x</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></munderover><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>The inner product allows the rigorous introduction of the intuitive geometrical notion of the angle between two vectors, and to define the cosine similarity (Equation (<a href="#FD10-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link">A1</a>)). Now we consider the Mahalanobis metric [<a href="#B28-metabolites-10-00435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-metabolites-10-00435">28</a>], defined as: </p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD14-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm125" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mi>T</mi></msup><msup><mi>C</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A5)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>C</em> is a generic covariance matrix. Since any covariance matrix <em>C</em> is always semi-positive defined, then, the Mahalanobis metric also satisfies the parallelogram equality, and the formula for the inner product can be rigorously derived:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula18"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="mm126" display="block"><mrow><mtable displaystyle="true"><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>−</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mi>i</mi></msub><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mspace width="1.em"></mspace></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>x</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>x</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>4</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mspace width="1.em"></mspace></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></munderover><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>≡</mo><msub><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi></msub><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>Therefore, the Mahalanobis metric induces an inner product space and an associated cosine measure:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD15-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm127" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>M</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mrow><mo>〈</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>〉</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi></msub><mrow><msub><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi></msub><msub><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">y</mi><mo>|</mo><mo>|</mo></mrow><mi>M</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></msubsup><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mrow><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt><msqrt><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>m</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><msubsup><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><msubsup><mi>λ</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm128"><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow></math></span> are the elements of the vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm129"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow></math></span> in the base <em>U</em> where the covariance matrix is diagonal with eigenvalues <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm130"><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD16-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm131" display="block"><mrow><mrow><mi>Λ</mi><mo>=</mo><msup><mi>U</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>C</mi><mi>U</mi><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="(" close=")"><mtable><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mtd><mtd><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mo>⋮</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>⋱</mo></mtd><mtd><mo>⋮</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>⋯</mo></mtd><mtd><msub><mi>λ</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A7)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="app2-metabolites-10-00435" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Appendix B. Data Processing Pipeline</h2>
<p>All raw data sets come in the form of profile vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm132"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">w</mi></mrow></math></span> containing concentration values of <em>M</em> biological signatures:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD17-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm133" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>M</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>g</em> is the mutant index and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm134"><mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>g</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the replicate index, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm135"><mrow><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>g</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> being the number of replicates of mutant <em>g</em>. Gene deletion profiles are mostly in replicates of four in the ionome data sets, while in the metabolome data set, only a single amino acid profile is available for most mutants. To pre-process the raw data, we perform the following operations:</p>
<p><em>Log-transformation.</em> A logarithmic transformation is applied to all the profile vectors:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD18-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm136" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mover accent="false"><mi mathvariant="bold">w</mi><mo stretchy="false">˜</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mi>l</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>g</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><em>Median batch normalisation.</em> Each profile element is normalised by the median value of all the concentration values of the corresponding signature <em>i</em> measured in the same <em>p</em> plate (ionome data) or <em>p</em> batch (metabolome data). If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm137"><mrow><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is the ensamble of mutants measured on plate/batch <em>p</em>, then:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD19-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm138" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">˜</mo></mover><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>−</mo><mi>median</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mo>{</mo><msubsup><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">˜</mo></mover><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>}</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><em>Outlier detection and removal.</em> All normalised concentration values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm139"><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> larger than three or smaller than −3 (corresponding to an absolute log fold-change of 3 with respect to the median value of their plate/batch) are labelled as outliers. These extreme concentration values are unlikely to have any biological explanation and are reasonably considered to result from technical (e.g., mass spectrometer performance) or methodological (e.g., sample preparation) error. All profiles containing at least one outlier value are removed from the data set.</p>

<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD20-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm140" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>g</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mo>[</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>g</mi></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>]</mo></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>∀</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A11)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><em>Standardisation.</em> Each profile element <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm141"><mrow><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover></mrow></math></span> is then standardised by the standard deviation of all concentration values measured across all mutants and replicates:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD21-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm142" display="block"><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>σ</mi><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover></msub></mfrac></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(A12)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
were the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm143"><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover></msub></mrow></math></span> are robustly estimated using the mean absolute deviation, through the formula <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm144"><mrow><mrow><mi>mad</mi><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mo>{</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mover><mi>i</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>}</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo><mo>·</mo><mn>1.235</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>

<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD22-metabolites-10-00435"><tr>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f0A3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Distribution of the final <em>z</em>-scores across all mutants of the Metabolome AA data set for each element in the data set. All distributions exhibit long tails and are highly skewed.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="app3-metabolites-10-00435" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Appendix C. Synthetic Data Sets</h2>
<p>To systematically study the effect of the features’ correlation and that of the features’ skewness on the discrepancy between the profile similarity scores computed with the Pearson’s coefficient and those computed with the cosine similarity we generated synthetic data sets of <em>n</em> = 300 profiles of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm146"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> features. To obtain a desired level of correlation between the features, we adopted the following procedure:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>Step (1): We constructed a square <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm147"><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> correlation matrix <em>A</em> with ones on the main diagonal and the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm148"><mrow><mrow><mo>[</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>]</mo><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> elements of the upper triangular matrix sampled uniformly at random within a certain correlation interval (e.g., if we want high correlation level, within the interval [0.9,1]). The elements of the lower triangular matrix are imputed from the upper triangular matrix so to have <em>A</em> symmetric.</p></li>
<li><p>Step (2): As the eigenvalues of <em>A</em> are required to be greater than zero, we computed <em>S</em> as the nearest positive definite to the correlation matrix <em>A</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>Step (3): We derived the lower triangular <em>L</em> matrix of <em>S</em> via Cholesky decomposition so to have <em>S</em> = <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm149"><mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi><mi>L</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>′.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>At this point, it is possible to generate a set of <em>n</em> observation of <em>M</em> multivariate Normal correlated features with zero means via the matrix product <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm150"><mrow><mrow><mi>Z</mi><mi>L</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> between an <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm151"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> matrix <em>Z</em> of <em>M</em> random N (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm152"><mrow><mi>μ</mi></mrow></math></span> = 0, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm153"><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow></math></span> = 1) i.i.d. features, and the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm154"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> matrix <em>L</em>. The resulting correlation range of the simulated features will be close enough to those assigned to the matrix <em>A</em>. In an analogous way, we also generated synthetic correlated log-Normal features for different levels of feature–feature correlation, for which the empirical distribution of each feature is skewed (by tuning the standard deviation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm155"><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow></math></span> of the log-Normal marginal distribution of <em>Z</em>, it is possible to have for each feature skewness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm156"><mrow><mrow><mo>≥</mo><mn>1.5</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>).</p>
<p>In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A4" class="usa-link">Figure A4</a>, we report the scatter plots of the profile similarity scores obtained with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm157"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> against those obtained with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm158"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> on different synthetic data sets. In the top figures, we show the case of Normal (non-skewed) correlated features for low (range [0,0.1]), intermediate (range [0.5,0.6]), and high (range [0.9,1]) correlation levels, while in the bottom figures, we show the case of log-Normal (skewed) correlated features for the same correlation ranges. In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A5" class="usa-link">Figure A5</a>B, we plot the trend of the root–mean–square error (RMSE) of the scores measured with the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm159"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> with respect to the scores measured with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm160"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, in function of the correlation range of <em>A</em>. As expected, the higher the level of correlation between the features, the more we observe some of the scores returned by the Pearson coefficient and by the cosine similarity to differ. Moreover, the skewness of the features works as an amplifying factor for the RMSE up to relatively high levels (∼0.8) of features’ correlation. In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A5" class="usa-link">Figure A5</a>A, we show that this discrepancy in the score values is indeed related to the term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm161"><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> that appears in the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm162"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> formula. By increasing the level of feature correlation, the variance of the distribution of the average profile values across the <em>n</em> observations (centred around <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm163"><mrow><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>) also increases, so that the fraction of profiles for which <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm164"><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo></mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup><mrow><mo>|</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span> grows. When the features are extracted from broader, skewed distributions (log-Normal features), the increase in the feature correlation level produces an elongation in the tail of the distribution for positive <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm165"><mrow><msup><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>¯</mo></mover><mi>g</mi></msup></mrow></math></span> values that contributes to further increasing the fraction of pairs of profiles <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm166"><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>g</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> for which <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm167"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>≄</mo><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><mi>g</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow><msup><mi>g</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span> compared to the case of Normal features.</p>

<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7693762_metabolites-10-00435-g0A6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/86b8/7693762/f368831652cb/metabolites-10-00435-g0A6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="273" width="760" alt="Figure A6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-10-00435-f0A6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Scatter plots of the profile similarity scores obtained with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm180"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>C</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> against those obtained with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm181"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> for the three experimental data sets used as benchmarks in this study.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="app4-metabolites-10-00435" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Appendix D. False Positive Rate of Genetic Associations</h2>
<p>In <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a> and <a href="#metabolites-10-00435-f0A7" class="usa-link">Figure A7</a>, we show the true positive rate (TPR) of associations between genes for the measures and the data sets under study with respect to selected ground-truth association sets from databases. A similar analysis to estimate the false positive rate (FPR) would be limiting because the FPR would be relative to each ground truth annotation set. One approach would be to aggregate all the ground truth annotations into a single set; however, the resulting FPR would still implicitly assume that the universe of all biological associations were contained in the aggregated set and it would disregard any possible newly discovered association. Therefore, the FPRs was estimated from the null distributions of the similarity measures by randomly permuting the elements of each column of the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm182"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> matrices of profiles independently. This was repeated 30 times. After each permutation round, the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm183"><mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> correlation matrix was extracted for each of the measures under study. Once the 30-permutations cycle was completed, all the correlation values related to a specific measure were used to construct a null distribution of the similarity scores for each measure. In order to calculate empirical <em>p</em>-values from the null distributions, the similarity threshold discriminating the top <em>n</em>-th similarity values for each of the measures was retrieved from the original analysis and the percentages of values in the null distributions that were above those thresholds was computed. The <em>p</em>-values (<a href="#metabolites-10-00435-t0A2" class="usa-link">Table A2</a>) indicate the maximum percentage of associations found the networks that are consistent with the null distributions of similarity scores, and, therefore, they represent an upper bound for the false positive rate of associations.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-10-00435-t0A2"><h3 class="obj_head">Table A2.</h3>
<div class="caption p"><p>The maximum false positive rates of genetic associations estimated using empirical <em>p</em>-values from the null distributions of the similarity scores. The null distributions are computed through random permutations of the profile matrices for each data set under study.</p></div>
